# English Form 3, Form Three English: drafts for review (Lessons 38 lessons (2–39))

Generated by `npm run review:f3-english` from `content/form-3/english/*.json`. Every question and drawing below was built by the engine.
All notes, teach cards, worked examples and paper tasks are **status: draft**. “Authored key” means a person fixed the
right answer (see `AUTHORED.md`); “computed by rule” means code worked it out (a formula, a conversion, a spelling rule).

Teach mode: cards (idea + example + 1 level-1 check) → worked example → practice (levels 1–3) → the note as a summary.

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _obligation: an action required in the stated setting._

> _prohibition: an action the rule forbids._  
> _reason: an explanation supporting a rule._

> Which term means: an explanation supporting a rule ?

- ◻️ permission

- ◻️ prohibition  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: prohibition means an action the rule forbids; the question instead describes an explanation supporting a rule._
- ✅ reason
- ◻️ obligation  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: obligation means an action required in the stated setting; the question instead describes an explanation supporting a rule._

Full working after a second miss:

> An explanation supporting a rule.  
> So the answer is “reason”.

**Summary (the note)**

> A rule states what is required, allowed or prohibited in a setting. Use clear language and explain the reason when discussing it. Distinguish must for obligation, may for permission and must not for prohibition. A respectful discussion can ask how a rule protects people or supports shared work. A rule for one setting should not be assumed to describe every household or school. Practise the dialogue in writing before speaking with a partner; this app does not assess your voice.

## Lesson 2: Speaking & Reading - Talk about dos and don'ts at school and at home

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-2 (Discussing rules at school and home)

**Note** (draft)

> A rule states what is required, allowed or prohibited in a setting. Use clear language and explain the reason when discussing it. Distinguish must for obligation, may for permission and must not for prohibition. A respectful discussion can ask how a rule protects people or supports shared work. A rule for one setting should not be assumed to describe every household or school. Practise the dialogue in writing before speaking with a partner; this app does not assess your voice.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> A rule states what is required, allowed or prohibited in a setting. Use clear language and explain the reason when discussing it. Distinguish must for obligation, may for permission and must not for prohibition. A respectful discussion can ask how a rule protects people or supports shared work. A rule for one setting should not be assumed to describe every household or school. Practise the dialogue in writing before speaking with a partner; this app does not assess your voice.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original dialogue: Bih asks why pupils must return library books. Ayuk explains that others need to borrow them. Bih asks whether she may renew a loan. The librarian says she may request a renewal before the due date. The exchange gives a rule, a reason and a polite request rather than merely an angry command.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Identify the setting and the action before choosing a modal. When sharing a view, refer to the rule rather than insulting a person. Distinguish explaining a rule from claiming that every rule is beyond discussion. Keep requests polite and specific.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **obligation**: an action required in the stated setting
- **permission**: an action allowed in the stated setting
- **prohibition**: an action the rule forbids
- **reason**: an explanation supporting a rule

- A notice says pupils must return borrowed books.
- The librarian says a pupil may ask for a renewal.
- A notice says users must not tear pages.

Common mistake: “Must return means returning is entirely optional.” → “obligation: an action required in the stated setting.”. Must marks a requirement here, not an optional suggestion.

## Lesson 3: Grammar - Modals expressing obligation, advice and polite requests

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-3 (Modals: obligation, advice and polite requests)

**Note** (draft)

> Must and have to can express obligation; should usually gives advice. Could you and would you can introduce polite requests. A modal is followed by the base verb: she must read, not must reads or must to read. Must not means prohibition; do not have to means absence of necessity. Context matters because a word can have more than one use. State the intended meaning rather than treating all modals as interchangeable.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> Must and have to can express obligation; should usually gives advice. Could you and would you can introduce polite requests. A modal is followed by the base verb: she must read, not must reads or must to read. Must not means prohibition; do not have to means absence of necessity. Context matters because a word can have more than one use. State the intended meaning rather than treating all modals as interchangeable.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> At a Buea library, Bih must return the book by the agreed date. A friend says she should keep a reading list: that is advice. Bih asks, Could you explain the renewal rule? This is a request, not a statement about past ability. A notice saying visitors do not have to buy a book does not forbid buying one.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Choose the meaning first, then the form. Check the base verb after a modal. When correcting a sentence, preserve whether the speaker intended a requirement, a suggestion, permission or prohibition. Do not replace must not with do not have to: that changes the meaning.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **must + base verb**: a form expressing obligation in these examples
- **should**: a modal expressing advice in this context
- **polite request**: a courteous request using a form such as Could you
- **absence of necessity**: the meaning of do not have to

- She must return the book.
- You should make a reading plan.
- Could you repeat the instruction, please?

Common mistake: “She must returns the book.” → “must + base verb: a form expressing obligation in these examples.”. Use the base return after must, without a third-person -s.

**Card ce3-3-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _must + base verb: a form expressing obligation in these examples._  
> _should: a modal expressing advice in this context._  
> _polite request: a courteous request using a form such as Could you._  
> _absence of necessity: the meaning of do not have to._  

Check question: `e3-3-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

- ◻️ should  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: should means a modal expressing advice in this context; the question instead describes the meaning of do not have to._
- ◻️ polite request  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: polite request means a courteous request using a form such as Could you; the question instead describes the meaning of do not have to._
- ✅ absence of necessity
- ◻️ must + base verb  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: must + base verb means a form expressing obligation in these examples; the question instead describes the meaning of do not have to._

Full working after a second miss:

> The meaning of do not have to.  
> So the answer is “absence of necessity”.  

**Card ce3-3-cases** (draft)

> Classify a case by its features, rather than by a familiar word alone.
>
> _She must return the book._  
> _must + base verb: a form expressing obligation in these examples._  

Check question: `e3-3-cases` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Could you repeat the instruction, please?
> Which term best fits this example?

- ✅ polite request
- ◻️ absence of necessity

## Lesson 4: Writing - Write school rules and regulations

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-4 (Writing school rules and regulations)

**Note** (draft)

> A useful rule names the setting, the action and whether it is required or prohibited. Keep one main instruction in each statement. Use must or must not consistently, or an appropriate imperative. Rules should be clear, respectful and practical rather than vague threats. Explain a purpose where it helps. A set of classroom rules needs consistent presentation and should not pretend to be an official policy unless the school has approved it.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> A useful rule names the setting, the action and whether it is required or prohibited. Keep one main instruction in each statement. Use must or must not consistently, or an appropriate imperative. Rules should be clear, respectful and practical rather than vague threats. Explain a purpose where it helps. A set of classroom rules needs consistent presentation and should not pretend to be an official policy unless the school has approved it.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original model: Library rules: Return borrowed books by the agreed date. Keep food away from the books. Speak quietly so others can read. Ask the librarian before moving reference materials.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Plan the behaviours first. Turn each into a clear instruction and check that positive and negative wording match the intended meaning. Read the set for contradictions. The exercise creates a draft in an exercise book, not a new school policy.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **clear action**: a specific behaviour named in a rule
- **prohibition**: a rule stating what must not be done
- **purpose**: the reason supporting a rule
- **consistency**: rules fitting together without contradictory instructions

- Return the book by the agreed date.
- Do not tear pages.
- Speak quietly so others can read.

Common mistake: “Be good is always as precise as a named action.” → “clear action: a specific behaviour named in a rule.”. A named behaviour gives the reader a clearer instruction than a vague judgment.

**Card ce3-4-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _clear action: a specific behaviour named in a rule._  
> _prohibition: a rule stating what must not be done._  
> _purpose: the reason supporting a rule._  
> _consistency: rules fitting together without contradictory instructions._  

Check question: `e3-4-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

- ◻️ purpose  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: purpose means the reason supporting a rule; the question instead describes a specific behaviour named in a rule._
- ◻️ prohibition  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: prohibition means a rule stating what must not be done; the question instead describes a specific behaviour named in a rule._
- ◻️ consistency  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: consistency means rules fitting together without contradictory instructions; the question instead describes a specific behaviour named in a rule._

Full working after a second miss:

> A specific behaviour named in a rule.  
> So the answer is “clear action”.

## Lesson 5: Speaking & Listening - Discuss and share personal study experiences. Listen to a text on strategies for studying

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-5 (Discussing ways of studying)

**Note** (draft)

> A useful discussion develops a suggestion with a reason, listens to another view and reaches a practical decision. Active recall means trying to retrieve information before checking a source; rereading means looking over it again. Both can have a role, but recognising a sentence is not the same as being able to explain it unaided. This is written preparation for speaking and listening; the screen cannot assess a learner's voice or listening comprehension.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> A useful discussion develops a suggestion with a reason, listens to another view and reaches a practical decision. Active recall means trying to retrieve information before checking a source; rereading means looking over it again. Both can have a role, but recognising a sentence is not the same as being able to explain it unaided. This is written preparation for speaking and listening; the screen cannot assess a learner's voice or listening comprehension.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original dialogue: Bih says, I remember definitions while reading but cannot explain them later. Ayuk replies, Try closing the book and explaining one idea, then check what you missed. Bih asks, Could we practise together after chores? Ayuk agrees to alternate questions and give reasons for corrections. Their plan includes practice and feedback rather than copying answers.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> State a difficulty, suggest an action and explain why it may help. Invite a response with a specific question. When preparing a spoken exchange, write short turns and rehearse with a partner if available. Disagreement should address the method, not insult the speaker.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **active recall**: retrieving an idea before checking the source
- **clarifying question**: a question seeking a clearer explanation
- **reasoned suggestion**: a proposed action supported by an explanation
- **constructive feedback**: a correction explaining what to improve

- Bih closes the book and explains a definition from memory.
- Could you show how to check a recalled answer?
- Try explaining aloud because it can reveal gaps.

Common mistake: “Recognising a sentence while looking at it is always unaided recall.” → “active recall: retrieving an idea before checking the source.”. Recall requires attempting retrieval without the answer in view.

**Card ce3-5-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _active recall: retrieving an idea before checking the source._  
> _clarifying question: a question seeking a clearer explanation._  
> _reasoned suggestion: a proposed action supported by an explanation._  
> _constructive feedback: a correction explaining what to improve._  

Check question: `e3-5-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means: a proposed action supported by an explanation ?

- ✅ reasoned suggestion
- ◻️ constructive feedback  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: constructive feedback means a correction explaining what to improve; the question instead describes a proposed action supported by an explanation._
- ◻️ active recall  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: active recall means retrieving an idea before checking the source; the question instead describes a proposed action supported by an explanation._
- ◻️ clarifying question  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: clarifying question means a question seeking a clearer explanation; the question instead describes a proposed action supported by an explanation._

Full working after a second miss:

> A proposed action supported by an explanation.  
> So the answer is “reasoned suggestion”.  

**Card ce3-5-cases** (draft)

> Classify a case by its features, rather than by a familiar word alone.
>
> _Bih closes the book and explains a definition from memory._  
> _active recall: retrieving an idea before checking the source._  

Check question: `e3-5-cases` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Try explaining aloud because it can reveal gaps.
> Which term best fits this example?

- ◻️ constructive feedback  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: constructive feedback concerns a correction explaining what to improve. This case instead illustrates a proposed action supported by an explanation._
- ◻️ clarifying question  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: clarifying question concerns a question seeking a clearer explanation. This case instead illustrates a proposed action supported by an explanation._
- ◻️ active recall  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: active recall concerns retrieving an idea before checking the source. This case instead illustrates a proposed action supported by an explanation._
- ✅ reasoned suggestion

Full working after a second miss:

> Try explaining aloud because it can reveal gaps. A proposed action supported by an explanation.  
> So the answer is “reasoned suggestion”.  

**Card ce3-5-repair** (draft)

> Check that a claim agrees with the rule or definition it uses.
>
> _Recognising a sentence while looking at it is always unaided recall._  
> _Recall requires attempting retrieval without the answer in view._  

Check question: `e3-5-repair-card` · spot the error, level 1 · **authored key**

> Find the incorrect claim.

- ✅ A suggestion needs no explanation even when asked why it may help.  
  _↳ explanation: A reason connects the proposed action with its purpose._
- ◻️ active recall: retrieving an idea before checking the source.  
  _↳ explanation: This statement is correct: active recall means retrieving an idea before checking the source. Look for a claim that conflicts with its definition._
- ◻️ constructive feedback: a correction explaining what to improve.  
  _↳ explanation: This statement is correct: constructive feedback means a correction explaining what to improve. Look for a claim that conflicts with its definition._
- ◻️ reasoned suggestion: a proposed action supported by an explanation.  
  _↳ explanation: This statement is correct: reasoned suggestion means a proposed action supported by an explanation. Look for a claim that conflicts with its definition._

Full working after a second miss:

> The wrong statement is “A suggestion needs no explanation even when asked why it may help.”.  
> A reason connects the proposed action with its purpose.  

**Worked example** (draft)

**Answer:** active recall

## Lesson 6: Writing - Draw a personal study timetable

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-6 (A personal study timetable)

**Note** (draft)

> A timetable organises activities into realistic periods. Begin with fixed commitments, including school, travel, chores, meals and rest. Add study periods that match your priorities and available energy. Allow transitions and breaks. A timetable is a plan to review, not a claim that every learner has identical responsibilities or resources. Use headings and consistent time notation so another person can understand it.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> A timetable organises activities into realistic periods. Begin with fixed commitments, including school, travel, chores, meals and rest. Add study periods that match your priorities and available energy. Allow transitions and breaks. A timetable is a plan to review, not a claim that every learner has identical responsibilities or resources. Use headings and consistent time notation so another person can understand it.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original model: Draft schedule: After the journey home, I complete the agreed household task. I then revise one topic, take a short break and practise questions. After the evening meal, I check the next day's books. I leave time for rest and adjust the plan when a task takes longer.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> List fixed commitments before choosing optional tasks. Check that periods do not overlap and that the total fits the available time. Review the plan after trying it; do not promise that a timetable alone guarantees success.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **time slot**: a stated period assigned to an activity
- **priority**: an activity given importance when time is limited
- **buffer**: unallocated allowance for delays or transitions
- **review**: checking and adjusting a plan after use

- A learner sets aside an interval after chores for reading.
- Bih allocates revision time before optional recreation.
- The plan allows time to move from a chore to study.

Common mistake: “A timetable needs no indication of when an activity happens.” → “time slot: a stated period assigned to an activity.”. Time slots make the plan usable rather than an unsequenced wish list.

**Card ce3-6-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _time slot: a stated period assigned to an activity._  
> _priority: an activity given importance when time is limited._  
> _buffer: unallocated allowance for delays or transitions._  
> _review: checking and adjusting a plan after use._

- ◻️ buffer  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: buffer means unallocated allowance for delays or transitions; the question instead describes checking and adjusting a plan after use._

Full working after a second miss:

> So the answer is “review”.

## Lesson 7: Grammar- Adverbs of frequency

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-7 (Adverbs of frequency)

**Note** (draft)

> Adverbs of frequency say how often an action happens. Always indicates every relevant occasion; usually and often indicate frequent occurrence; sometimes is less regular; never means no occasions. Their exact numerical frequency is not fixed. A common position is before an ordinary main verb but after be: Bih often reads; Bih is often early. Some adverbs allow other positions. Do not mark a different standard position wrong merely because it is not the model's preferred pattern.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> Adverbs of frequency say how often an action happens. Always indicates every relevant occasion; usually and often indicate frequent occurrence; sometimes is less regular; never means no occasions. Their exact numerical frequency is not fixed. A common position is before an ordinary main verb but after be: Bih often reads; Bih is often early. Some adverbs allow other positions. Do not mark a different standard position wrong merely because it is not the model's preferred pattern.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Bih usually studies after chores, but sometimes she studies at the library. Ayuk is often early for the reading club. These sentences describe habits, not how fast or carefully the learners work. A timetable can help a learner report a habit accurately rather than claiming always after one observed occasion.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Ask whether the word concerns frequency, manner or time. For the simple pattern, place it before an ordinary main verb and after be. Keep the meaning when changing word order. Never is already negative in standard school usage: avoid adding an unnecessary not.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **frequency**: how often something happens
- **before the main verb**: the common position in a simple affirmative sentence
- **after be**: the common position with a simple form of be
- **never**: a frequency adverb meaning on no occasions

- Bih often visits the library.
- Ayuk usually reads after supper.
- Bih is often early.

Common mistake: “Often tells us how fast Bih walks.” → “frequency: how often something happens.”. Often concerns repetition, not speed or manner.

**Card ce3-7-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _frequency: how often something happens._  
> _before the main verb: the common position in a simple affirmative sentence._  
> _after be: the common position with a simple form of be._  
> _never: a frequency adverb meaning on no occasions._  

Check question: `e3-7-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means: the common position in a simple affirmative sentence ?

- ◻️ frequency  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: frequency means how often something happens; the question instead describes the common position in a simple affirmative sentence._
- ◻️ never  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: never means a frequency adverb meaning on no occasions; the question instead describes the common position in a simple affirmative sentence._
- ◻️ after be  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: after be means the common position with a simple form of be; the question instead describes the common position in a simple affirmative sentence._
- ✅ before the main verb

Full working after a second miss:

> The common position in a simple affirmative sentence.  
> So the answer is “before the main verb”.  

**Card ce3-7-cases** (draft)

## Lesson 8: Speech Work- The sounds /ai/ and /aʊ/

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-8 (Written preparation: /aɪ/ and /aʊ/)

**Note** (draft)

> A diphthong is a vowel sound that moves between positions within a syllable. Phonetic symbols represent sounds rather than ordinary letter names. This lesson groups written model words; it does not play audio or test pronunciation. Accent conventions vary: the GOAT vowel may be transcribed differently in other standard models. Use the teacher's agreed model and a reliable recording to practise actual speech.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> A diphthong is a vowel sound that moves between positions within a syllable. Phonetic symbols represent sounds rather than ordinary letter names. This lesson groups written model words; it does not play audio or test pronunciation. Accent conventions vary: the GOAT vowel may be transcribed differently in other standard models. Use the teacher's agreed model and a reliable recording to practise actual speech.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Model groups: time → /aɪ/; light → /aɪ/; house (noun) → /aʊ/; mouth (noun) → /aʊ/. A sound can have different spellings. A spelling can also have different sounds, so a letter rule cannot replace checking an unfamiliar word. Read the symbols as labels for the models, not as an instruction to pronounce two separate syllables.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Locate the relevant vowel in a model word, compare its transcription and group the written examples. Then practise the sound with a teacher or recording when available. Keep written recognition separate from an actual listening assessment.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **time**: a model word containing /aɪ/ and written as time
- **light**: a model word containing /aɪ/ and written as light
- **house (noun)**: a model word containing /aʊ/ and written as house (noun)
- **mouth (noun)**: a model word containing /aʊ/ and written as mouth (noun)

- time is placed in the /aɪ/ group in this written model.
- light is placed in the /aɪ/ group in this written model.
- house (noun) is placed in the /aʊ/ group in this written model.

Common mistake: “The spelling of time alone proves how every similar-looking word sounds.” → “time: a model word containing /aɪ/ and written as time.”. Spelling patterns have exceptions; confirm the model with a teacher or dictionary recording.

**Card ce3-8-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _time: a model word containing /aɪ/ and written as time._  
> _light: a model word containing /aɪ/ and written as light._  
> _house (noun): a model word containing /aʊ/ and written as house (noun)._  
> _mouth (noun): a model word containing /aʊ/ and written as mouth (noun)._

## Lesson 9: Speaking & Listening - Discuss strategies of taking care of the elderly, siblings, handicaps/sick etc

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-9 (Discussing care and respectful support)

**Note** (draft)

> Support begins by asking what a person needs and respecting their choices. Age, illness or disability does not remove a person's dignity or make every person need the same assistance. Discuss practical help, permission, clear communication and when to seek a responsible adult. This lesson teaches respectful English rather than medical care. Learners should not lift people, administer treatment or take over an adult's responsibilities as a classroom exercise.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> Support begins by asking what a person needs and respecting their choices. Age, illness or disability does not remove a person's dignity or make every person need the same assistance. Discuss practical help, permission, clear communication and when to seek a responsible adult. This lesson teaches respectful English rather than medical care. Learners should not lift people, administer treatment or take over an adult's responsibilities as a classroom exercise.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original dialogue: Ayuk asks an older neighbour, Would you like help carrying that light bag? The neighbour accepts and names where to put it. Bih asks a classmate which route is accessible rather than assuming what the classmate can do. If a younger sibling becomes unwell, a learner tells a responsible adult rather than guessing a treatment.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Choose wording that offers rather than imposes help. Listen to the answer and avoid speaking about a present person as though absent. In a discussion, distinguish an observation from an assumption and keep personal health details private.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **offer of help**: a request for permission to give practical support
- **respectful description**: language preserving a person's dignity
- **accessibility question**: a question about what makes participation possible
- **responsible referral**: seeking appropriate adult or professional support

- Would you like me to carry this light bag?
- A speaker asks a classmate what assistance would be useful.
- Which entrance is accessible for you?

Common mistake: “Offering help means ignoring a person's refusal.” → “offer of help: a request for permission to give practical support.”. An offer gives the person a choice.

**Card ce3-9-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _offer of help: a request for permission to give practical support._  
> _respectful description: language preserving a person's dignity._  
> _accessibility question: a question about what makes participation possible._  
> _responsible referral: seeking appropriate adult or professional support._

- ◻️ offer of help  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: offer of help means a request for permission to give practical support; the question instead describes a question about what makes participation possible._
- ◻️ respectful description  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: respectful description means language preserving a person's dignity; the question instead describes a question about what makes participation possible._

## Lesson 10: Writing- Informal Letter -Write a letter to/about family members

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-10 (An informal letter to a family member)

**Note** (draft)

> An informal letter communicates with someone you know personally. Include an appropriate greeting, a clear purpose, connected paragraphs and a suitable ending. A warm tone does not mean careless spelling or an unclear message. Ask questions relevant to the recipient and organise news so it can be followed. Do not copy private addresses or information into the app; use an imaginary sender and recipient for the exercise.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> An informal letter communicates with someone you know personally. Include an appropriate greeting, a clear purpose, connected paragraphs and a suitable ending. A warm tone does not mean careless spelling or an unclear message. Ask questions relevant to the recipient and organise news so it can be followed. Do not copy private addresses or information into the app; use an imaginary sender and recipient for the exercise.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original model: Dear Auntie, I hope you are well. Our reading club has started a shared notebook, and I have enjoyed choosing stories for it. I am learning to finish my chores before the study period. How is your garden growing? Please greet the family for me. With love, Bih.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Decide why you are writing before choosing the news to include. Give each paragraph a focus, keep pronouns clear and reread the tone from the recipient's position. A model is one possible answer, not a text to copy word for word.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **salutation**: a greeting addressed to the recipient
- **body**: the main message organised into paragraphs
- **appropriate tone**: language suited to the relationship and purpose
- **closing**: a suitable ending followed by the writer's name

- Dear Auntie, opens a personal letter.
- A paragraph explains the family news and asks a relevant question.
- A learner writes warmly to a familiar relative.

Common mistake: “The salutation is the main account of all events in the letter.” → “salutation: a greeting addressed to the recipient.”. The greeting opens the exchange; the body carries the detailed message.

**Card ce3-10-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _salutation: a greeting addressed to the recipient._  
> _body: the main message organised into paragraphs._  
> _appropriate tone: language suited to the relationship and purpose._  
> _closing: a suitable ending followed by the writer's name._  

Check question: `e3-10-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

- ✅ salutation
- ◻️ appropriate tone  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: appropriate tone means language suited to the relationship and purpose; the question instead describes a greeting addressed to the recipient._
- ◻️ closing  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: closing means a suitable ending followed by the writer's name; the question instead describes a greeting addressed to the recipient._

Full working after a second miss:

> So the answer is “salutation”.

## Lesson 11: Speaking & Reading - Share views on common teenage problems in the school milieu/society

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-11 (Discussing difficulties faced by teenagers)

**Note** (draft)

> A discussion can describe pressures, explain a possible cause and suggest support without making a diagnosis. Use may or can for possibilities rather than declaring that every teenager has the same experience. A speaker can disagree respectfully and ask for clarification. Use fictional situations; nobody must disclose private experiences. When a problem concerns harm or serious distress, encourage help from a trusted adult or appropriate trained service rather than relying only on classmates.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> A discussion can describe pressures, explain a possible cause and suggest support without making a diagnosis. Use may or can for possibilities rather than declaring that every teenager has the same experience. A speaker can disagree respectfully and ask for clarification. Use fictional situations; nobody must disclose private experiences. When a problem concerns harm or serious distress, encourage help from a trusted adult or appropriate trained service rather than relying only on classmates.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original dialogue: Bih says an imaginary pupil feels pulled between homework and household duties. Ayuk asks which tasks have fixed deadlines. They suggest discussing a realistic schedule with a responsible adult. Another speaker notes that a schedule may help time pressure but cannot solve every problem. Each turn develops the case without judging the pupil's character.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Separate the difficulty, evidence, possible cause and proposed support. Avoid generalisations such as all teenagers are lazy. Give a reason for a suggestion and recognise its limits. Prepare questions on paper before a partner discussion; the app checks written choices, not spoken performance.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **qualified claim**: a statement expressing a possibility without claiming universality
- **clarification**: a request for information needed to understand a difficulty
- **supported suggestion**: an action connected to the stated problem
- **respectful disagreement**: challenging an idea without attacking a person

- Time pressure may affect this pupil's concentration.
- Which tasks have fixed deadlines?
- Discuss a feasible timetable because the deadlines conflict.

Common mistake: “May proves the same cause applies to every teenager.” → “qualified claim: a statement expressing a possibility without claiming universality.”. May signals possibility rather than certainty for everyone.

**Card ce3-11-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _qualified claim: a statement expressing a possibility without claiming universality._  
> _clarification: a request for information needed to understand a difficulty._  
> _supported suggestion: an action connected to the stated problem._  
> _respectful disagreement: challenging an idea without attacking a person._  

Check question: `e3-11-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means: a statement expressing a possibility without claiming universality ?

- ◻️ respectful disagreement  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: respectful disagreement means challenging an idea without attacking a person; the question instead describes a statement expressing a possibility without claiming universality._
- ◻️ supported suggestion  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: supported suggestion means an action connected to the stated problem; the question instead describes a statement expressing a possibility without claiming universality._
- ◻️ clarification  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: clarification means a request for information needed to understand a difficulty; the question instead describes a statement expressing a possibility without claiming universality._
- ✅ qualified claim

Full working after a second miss:

> A statement expressing a possibility without claiming universality.  
> So the answer is “qualified claim”.

## Lesson 12: Grammar - Connectives expressing addition

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-12 (Connectives expressing addition)

**Note** (draft)

> Addition connectives link information without necessarily showing a cause or contrast. And joins similar grammatical units. Also commonly adds a point within a clause. Moreover and furthermore can add a further point between clauses or sentences. As well as adds related information, but its structure is not always interchangeable with and. Keep parallel forms and suitable punctuation. A word such as because introduces a reason, not merely an additional point.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> Addition connectives link information without necessarily showing a cause or contrast. And joins similar grammatical units. Also commonly adds a point within a clause. Moreover and furthermore can add a further point between clauses or sentences. As well as adds related information, but its structure is not always interchangeable with and. Keep parallel forms and suitable punctuation. A word such as because introduces a reason, not merely an additional point.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> The school club reads stories and discusses them. It also keeps a reading journal. Moreover, members help younger pupils choose books. Each sentence adds information about the club. Replacing moreover with therefore would claim a result relationship that the writer has not established.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Ask what relationship the writer intends. Link words to words and clauses to clauses without creating fragments. Do not use a comma alone to join full sentences simply because an addition adverb appears. Read the result aloud with a partner if possible; the written task checks structure, not pronunciation.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **and**: a coordinator adding parallel elements
- **also**: an adverb adding another point
- **furthermore**: a connector adding a further supporting point
- **because**: a connector giving a reason rather than simple addition

- The club reads and discusses stories.
- The club also keeps a journal.
- The room is quiet. Furthermore, it has useful books.

Common mistake: “The club reads and discussion stories.” → “and: a coordinator adding parallel elements.”. Join parallel forms: reads and discusses, not a verb and an unmatched noun.

**Card ce3-12-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _and: a coordinator adding parallel elements._  
> _also: an adverb adding another point._  
> _furthermore: a connector adding a further supporting point._  
> _because: a connector giving a reason rather than simple addition._  

Check question: `e3-12-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

- ◻️ also  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: also means an adverb adding another point; the question instead describes a connector giving a reason rather than simple addition._
- ◻️ and  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: and means a coordinator adding parallel elements; the question instead describes a connector giving a reason rather than simple addition._
- ◻️ furthermore  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: furthermore means a connector adding a further supporting point; the question instead describes a connector giving a reason rather than simple addition._
- ✅ because

Full working after a second miss:

> A connector giving a reason rather than simple addition.  
> So the answer is “because”.  

**Card ce3-12-cases** (draft)

> Classify a case by its features, rather than by a familiar word alone.

## Lesson 13: Writing - Formal letter - Write to an authority about the deviant behaviour in your school

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-13 (A formal letter about a school concern)

**Note** (draft)

> A formal letter addresses a person in an official role. State the purpose clearly, describe a specific concern and request a realistic response. Use a respectful tone and organised paragraphs. Distinguish observed facts from reports or opinions. Avoid naming classmates in unsupported accusations. School conventions for addresses, date, subject and closing should be confirmed by the teacher. This task uses an imaginary school and writer.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> A formal letter addresses a person in an official role. State the purpose clearly, describe a specific concern and request a realistic response. Use a respectful tone and organised paragraphs. Distinguish observed facts from reports or opinions. Avoid naming classmates in unsupported accusations. School conventions for addresses, date, subject and closing should be confirmed by the teacher. This task uses an imaginary school and writer.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original model: Dear Principal, I am writing about damage to materials in the shared reading room. During recent meetings, books have been left near an uncovered window. I request a dry storage shelf and a reminder about returning materials safely. These steps could help everyone use the books. Thank you for considering the request. Yours faithfully, Bih.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Identify the recipient, concern, evidence and proposed remedy. Remove insults and guesses presented as facts. Check the salutation and closing against the convention taught in class. A respectful request is stronger when its practical purpose is easy to identify.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **recipient's role**: the official function to which a request is addressed
- **subject line**: a concise statement of the letter's purpose
- **evidence-based concern**: a specific problem described without unsupported personal accusations
- **polite request**: a clear proposal expressed respectfully

- A learner addresses the principal about a school concern.
- Request for a safe place to store club books identifies the issue.
- A letter reports that books were left in the rain during a meeting.

Common mistake: “An official request needs no indication of who should deal with it.” → “recipient's role: the official function to which a request is addressed.”. The recipient's role helps direct the request appropriately.

**Card ce3-13-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _recipient's role: the official function to which a request is addressed._  
> _subject line: a concise statement of the letter's purpose._  
> _evidence-based concern: a specific problem described without unsupported personal accusations._  
> _polite request: a clear proposal expressed respectfully._

- ◻️ evidence-based concern

- ◻️ recipient's role  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: recipient's role means the official function to which a request is addressed; the question instead describes a clear proposal expressed respectfully._

## Lesson 14: Speaking & Reading - share opinions about club activities

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-14 (Expressing opinions about school clubs)

**Note** (draft)

> An opinion states a view; a reason explains why the speaker holds it. An example makes the reason concrete. In a club discussion, acknowledge another view before responding and distinguish a preference from a checked fact. I prefer drama does not prove every pupil should choose it. Spoken discussion needs turn-taking and attentive listening; here you prepare the wording and read an original dialogue before practising with a partner.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> An opinion states a view; a reason explains why the speaker holds it. An example makes the reason concrete. In a club discussion, acknowledge another view before responding and distinguish a preference from a checked fact. I prefer drama does not prove every pupil should choose it. Spoken discussion needs turn-taking and attentive listening; here you prepare the wording and read an original dialogue before practising with a partner.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original dialogue: Bih says, I prefer the reading club because I enjoy discussing stories. Ayuk replies, I see your point, but the environmental club interests me because members organise useful projects. Bih asks, Could the clubs share an exhibition? They propose a joint activity instead of treating preferences as an argument about personal worth.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Build a turn from view, reason and example. Use I agree because or I see your point, but rather than repeating yes or no without explanation. Do not invent membership figures or activities for a real school. Use the fictional club in the model or information the teacher confirms.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **opinion**: a speaker's stated view or preference
- **supporting reason**: an explanation for the stated preference
- **acknowledgement**: recognition of another view before responding
- **proposal**: a suggested action for consideration

- I prefer the reading club.
- I enjoy it because members discuss stories.
- I see your point, but another club interests me.

Common mistake: “Every personal preference is a verified fact about all pupils.” → “opinion: a speaker's stated view or preference.”. A preference describes the speaker's view.

**Card ce3-14-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _opinion: a speaker's stated view or preference._  
> _supporting reason: an explanation for the stated preference._  
> _acknowledgement: recognition of another view before responding._  
> _proposal: a suggested action for consideration._  

Check question: `e3-14-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

  _↳ feedback if chosen: supporting reason means an explanation for the stated preference; the question instead describes a suggested action for consideration._
- ◻️ opinion  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: opinion means a speaker's stated view or preference; the question instead describes a suggested action for consideration._
- ◻️ acknowledgement  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: acknowledgement means recognition of another view before responding; the question instead describes a suggested action for consideration._
- ✅ proposal

Full working after a second miss:

> So the answer is “proposal”.

## Lesson 15: Grammar - Irregular adverbial. e.g. well-better-best

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-15 (Irregular comparison of adverbs)

**Note** (draft)

> Some adverbs form comparisons irregularly. Well becomes better and best; badly becomes worse and worst. Far can become farther or further, depending on the use; course conventions should be stated. A comparative relates performances or situations; a superlative identifies the highest or lowest within a group in the relevant respect. Do not add more to better or attach -er to well. Distinguish the adjective good from the adverb well when describing how an action is performed.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> Some adverbs form comparisons irregularly. Well becomes better and best; badly becomes worse and worst. Far can become farther or further, depending on the use; course conventions should be stated. A comparative relates performances or situations; a superlative identifies the highest or lowest within a group in the relevant respect. Do not add more to better or attach -er to well. Distinguish the adjective good from the adverb well when describing how an action is performed.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Bih reads well. After practice she reads better than before. In the stated group, she reads best. This compares a performance, not her worth as a person. A class should specify the comparison group and the criterion rather than treating best as a permanent label on a learner.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Find the verb being modified and the comparison intended. Use than when an explicit comparative clause needs it. Avoid double comparison such as more better. When several forms are standard, do not invent an incorrect rule just to force one option.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **well**: an adverb describing a good manner of performance
- **better**: the comparative form of well in this use
- **best**: the superlative form of well in this use
- **worse**: the comparative form of badly

- Bih reads well.
- Ayuk reads better than before.
- Of the stated performances, Ngozi reads best.

Common mistake: “Bih reads good is the neutral standard school adverb pattern.” → “well: an adverb describing a good manner of performance.”. Well normally modifies the action here; good is an adjective.

**Card ce3-15-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _well: an adverb describing a good manner of performance._  
> _better: the comparative form of well in this use._  
> _best: the superlative form of well in this use._  
> _worse: the comparative form of badly._  

Check question: `e3-15-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means: an adverb describing a good manner of performance ?

- ✅ well
- ◻️ best  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: best means the superlative form of well in this use; the question instead describes an adverb describing a good manner of performance._
- ◻️ better  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: better means the comparative form of well in this use; the question instead describes an adverb describing a good manner of performance._
- ◻️ worse  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: worse means the comparative form of badly; the question instead describes an adverb describing a good manner of performance._

Full working after a second miss:

> An adverb describing a good manner of performance.  
> So the answer is “well”.  

**Card ce3-15-cases** (draft)

> Classify a case by its features, rather than by a familiar word alone.
>
> _Bih reads well._  
> _well: an adverb describing a good manner of performance._

  _↳ feedback if chosen: well concerns an adverb describing a good manner of performance. This case instead illustrates the comparative form of badly._
- ◻️ best  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: best concerns the superlative form of well in this use. This case instead illustrates the comparative form of badly._

Full working after a second miss:

## Lesson 16: Speech Work- The sounds /ɔi/ and /əʊ/

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-16 (Written preparation: /ɔɪ/ and /əʊ/)

**Note** (draft)

> A diphthong is a vowel sound that moves between positions within a syllable. Phonetic symbols represent sounds rather than ordinary letter names. This lesson groups written model words; it does not play audio or test pronunciation. Accent conventions vary: the GOAT vowel may be transcribed differently in other standard models. Use the teacher's agreed model and a reliable recording to practise actual speech.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> A diphthong is a vowel sound that moves between positions within a syllable. Phonetic symbols represent sounds rather than ordinary letter names. This lesson groups written model words; it does not play audio or test pronunciation. Accent conventions vary: the GOAT vowel may be transcribed differently in other standard models. Use the teacher's agreed model and a reliable recording to practise actual speech.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Model groups: boy → /ɔɪ/; choice → /ɔɪ/; go → /əʊ/; home → /əʊ/. A sound can have different spellings. A spelling can also have different sounds, so a letter rule cannot replace checking an unfamiliar word. Read the symbols as labels for the models, not as an instruction to pronounce two separate syllables.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Locate the relevant vowel in a model word, compare its transcription and group the written examples. Then practise the sound with a teacher or recording when available. Keep written recognition separate from an actual listening assessment.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **boy**: a model word containing /ɔɪ/ and written as boy
- **choice**: a model word containing /ɔɪ/ and written as choice
- **go**: a model word containing /əʊ/ and written as go
- **home**: a model word containing /əʊ/ and written as home

- boy is placed in the /ɔɪ/ group in this written model.
- choice is placed in the /ɔɪ/ group in this written model.
- go is placed in the /əʊ/ group in this written model.

Common mistake: “The spelling of boy alone proves how every similar-looking word sounds.” → “boy: a model word containing /ɔɪ/ and written as boy.”. Spelling patterns have exceptions; confirm the model with a teacher or dictionary recording.

**Card ce3-16-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.

> _choice: a model word containing /ɔɪ/ and written as choice._  
> _go: a model word containing /əʊ/ and written as go._  
> _home: a model word containing /əʊ/ and written as home._

## Lesson 17: Writing - Descriptive -Describe activities carried out by a club in your school

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-17 (Describing a school club's activities)

**Note** (draft)

> A descriptive account helps the reader picture organised activities. Give the club's purpose, describe its setting and show what members do. Choose precise verbs and relevant details instead of an unconnected list. Use a consistent time frame and link the activities logically. Description can include small actions without becoming a full story about an unrelated journey. Avoid making claims about an actual school's club unless you have checked them.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> A descriptive account helps the reader picture organised activities. Give the club's purpose, describe its setting and show what members do. Choose precise verbs and relevant details instead of an unconnected list. Use a consistent time frame and link the activities logically. Description can include small actions without becoming a full story about an unrelated journey. Avoid making claims about an actual school's club unless you have checked them.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original model: Our imaginary reading club meets in a bright classroom. Members arrange the books on a long table and read in small groups. A volunteer writes new words on the board, while another records book loans. The quiet discussion helps learners explain their favourite passages. At the end, everyone returns the materials to their places.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Plan setting, participants and activities. Ask whether each detail helps the reader see the club at work. Replace vague words such as nice with a precise observation. Keep the tense and subject references consistent.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **setting**: the place and conditions in which activities occur
- **precise verb**: a verb naming a specific action
- **relevant detail**: an observation that develops the description's purpose
- **consistent tense**: verb forms fitting the chosen time frame

- The club works in a bright classroom with a long table.
- Members arrange books and record loans.
- A volunteer writes new words for the reading discussion.

Common mistake: “A setting is only the writer's signature.” → “setting: the place and conditions in which activities occur.”. Setting locates the activities for the reader.

**Card ce3-17-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _setting: the place and conditions in which activities occur._  
> _precise verb: a verb naming a specific action._  
> _relevant detail: an observation that develops the description's purpose._  
> _consistent tense: verb forms fitting the chosen time frame._  

Check question: `e3-17-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means: the place and conditions in which activities occur ?

- ◻️ consistent tense  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: consistent tense means verb forms fitting the chosen time frame; the question instead describes the place and conditions in which activities occur._
- ✅ setting
- ◻️ relevant detail  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: relevant detail means an observation that develops the description's purpose; the question instead describes the place and conditions in which activities occur._
- ◻️ precise verb  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: precise verb means a verb naming a specific action; the question instead describes the place and conditions in which activities occur._

Full working after a second miss:

> The place and conditions in which activities occur.  
> So the answer is “setting”.

> setting → The club works in a bright classroom with a long table.  
> consistent tense → The account describes regular meetings in the present.

## Lesson 18: Speaking & Reading - Compare and contrast table manners from different cultures. Read text on table manners

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-18 (Comparing table manners respectfully)

**Note** (draft)

> Customs around meals vary between households and occasions. A careful comparison describes a particular setting and avoids claiming that everyone in a country behaves identically. Use in this household, whereas and both to express similarities and differences. Ask a host politely when unsure. Language practice can consider courtesy, permission and appreciation without ranking whole cultures or requiring a pupil to reveal family practices.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> Customs around meals vary between households and occasions. A careful comparison describes a particular setting and avoids claiming that everyone in a country behaves identically. Use in this household, whereas and both to express similarities and differences. Ask a host politely when unsure. Language practice can consider courtesy, permission and appreciation without ranking whole cultures or requiring a pupil to reveal family practices.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original comparison: In Bih's fictional household, guests wait until the host invites them to begin. At an imaginary club picnic, participants serve themselves when the organiser announces the meal. Both settings encourage considerate behaviour. The difference concerns the stated occasion; it does not prove that one community is polite and another is not.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> State the setting before comparing. Use both for a similarity and whereas for a contrast. Avoid unsupported words such as always and everyone. A polite question about a local practice is more useful than judging it from an unfamiliar rule.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **limited setting**: the specific household or occasion described
- **similarity**: a feature shared by the stated settings
- **contrast**: a difference between the described practices
- **polite enquiry**: a courteous question about an unfamiliar practice

- In this fictional household, guests wait for an invitation.
- Both occasions encourage consideration for others.
- One meal is served by a host, whereas the picnic is self-service.

Common mistake: “One household establishes a rule for an entire country.” → “limited setting: the specific household or occasion described.”. A local example does not prove a national universal.

**Card ce3-18-terms** (draft)

> _similarity: a feature shared by the stated settings._  
> _contrast: a difference between the described practices._  
> _polite enquiry: a courteous question about an unfamiliar practice._

## Lesson 19: Grammar - Active and passive voice

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-19 (Active and passive voice)

**Note** (draft)

> In an active clause, the subject performs the action: Bih writes the notice. In a passive clause, the affected participant becomes the subject: the notice is written by Bih. A basic passive uses the appropriate form of be plus the past participle. Keep tense and number consistent. The agent can be omitted when unknown or unimportant. Not every verb can be passivised in the same way; use clear transitive examples with an object.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> In an active clause, the subject performs the action: Bih writes the notice. In a passive clause, the affected participant becomes the subject: the notice is written by Bih. A basic passive uses the appropriate form of be plus the past participle. Keep tense and number consistent. The agent can be omitted when unknown or unimportant. Not every verb can be passivised in the same way; use clear transitive examples with an object.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> The club cleans the room becomes the room is cleaned by the club. For a completed past action, Bih wrote the notice becomes the notice was written by Bih. Changing written to wrote after was confuses the past participle with the simple past. The passive changes focus; it does not automatically change who performed the event.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Identify subject, verb and object. Move the affected object to subject position, select be for the tense and number, then use the participle. Include by + agent only when useful. Check the result still describes the same action and time.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **active voice**: a clause whose subject performs the stated action
- **passive voice**: a clause focusing on an affected subject with be plus participle
- **agent**: the performer named in a passive by-phrase
- **tense preservation**: keeping the action's time when changing voice

- Bih writes the notice.
- The notice is written by Bih.
- In the notice was written by Ayuk, consider Ayuk's role.

Common mistake: “Every sentence containing an action word is necessarily passive.” → “active voice: a clause whose subject performs the stated action.”. Bih is the actor-subject here, so the clause is active.

**Card ce3-19-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _active voice: a clause whose subject performs the stated action._  
> _passive voice: a clause focusing on an affected subject with be plus participle._  
> _agent: the performer named in a passive by-phrase._  
> _tense preservation: keeping the action's time when changing voice._  

Check question: `e3-19-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

- ◻️ tense preservation  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: tense preservation means keeping the action's time when changing voice; the question instead describes a clause focusing on an affected subject with be plus participle._
- ◻️ agent  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: agent means the performer named in a passive by-phrase; the question instead describes a clause focusing on an affected subject with be plus participle._
- ◻️ active voice  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: active voice means a clause whose subject performs the stated action; the question instead describes a clause focusing on an affected subject with be plus participle._

Full working after a second miss:

> A clause focusing on an affected subject with be plus participle.  
> So the answer is “passive voice”.  

**Card ce3-19-cases** (draft)

> Classify a case by its features, rather than by a familiar word alone.
>
> _Bih writes the notice._  
> _active voice: a clause whose subject performs the stated action._

- ◻️ agent: the performer named in a passive by-phrase.  
  _↳ explanation: This statement is correct: agent means the performer named in a passive by-phrase. Look for a claim that conflicts with its definition._
- ✅ Every past active clause becomes a present passive clause.  
  _↳ explanation: A voice change should preserve the intended tense._

Full working after a second miss:

> The wrong statement is “Every past active clause becomes a present passive clause.”.  
> A voice change should preserve the intended tense.  

## Lesson 20: Writing - Describing a process - Preparing and serving a particular meal

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-20 (Describing a process: preparing and serving a meal)

**Note** (draft)

> A process account explains stages in a logical order. State the goal and what is needed, then describe the main steps with sequence words. Choose a consistent form, such as imperatives for instructions or present-simple sentences for an explanation. Include relevant hygiene and serving arrangements without pretending that one simple account is a complete food-safety manual. Do not use the task as permission to handle hot equipment without appropriate adult supervision.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> A process account explains stages in a logical order. State the goal and what is needed, then describe the main steps with sequence words. Choose a consistent form, such as imperatives for instructions or present-simple sentences for an explanation. Include relevant hygiene and serving arrangements without pretending that one simple account is a complete food-safety manual. Do not use the task as permission to handle hot equipment without appropriate adult supervision.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original model: To serve a simple family meal, first prepare a clean work area and gather the utensils. Next, follow an adult-approved recipe for the chosen dish. Keep serving materials clean and arrange the portions. Then invite the family and serve carefully. Finally, store or clear the remaining materials according to the household's safe routine.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Write the stages before drafting full sentences. Check that a reader could follow the order and that no required stage appears after the stage depending on it. Avoid unsupported cooking times or temperatures. The language task assesses the explanation's organisation.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **goal**: the intended result of a process
- **sequence**: the logical order of the stages
- **sequence marker**: a word or phrase showing a stage's position
- **consistent instruction form**: a stable grammatical pattern across the steps

- The account explains how to prepare and serve the stated meal.
- Gather utensils before using them.
- First, next and finally guide the account.

Common mistake: “A process needs no indication of what it is meant to achieve.” → “goal: the intended result of a process.”. The goal tells the reader what the stages lead toward.

**Card ce3-20-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _goal: the intended result of a process._  
> _sequence: the logical order of the stages._  
> _sequence marker: a word or phrase showing a stage's position._  
> _consistent instruction form: a stable grammatical pattern across the steps._  

Check question: `e3-20-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

- ◻️ sequence  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: sequence means the logical order of the stages; the question instead describes the intended result of a process._
- ◻️ sequence marker  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: sequence marker means a word or phrase showing a stage's position; the question instead describes the intended result of a process._
- ✅ goal

Full working after a second miss:

> The intended result of a process.  
> So the answer is “goal”.

## Lesson 21: Speaking & Reading - List the various professions and give the type of study needed for each

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-21 (Discussing professions and learning pathways)

**Note** (draft)

> An occupation is a kind of work. A duty is a task involved in it; a skill is an ability used to perform tasks; a qualification records a recognised educational or training achievement. These ideas are connected but not identical. Training routes and entry rules vary and may change. This lesson teaches how to ask about them, not current professional eligibility. Check exact requirements with the appropriate institution before making a real application.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> An occupation is a kind of work. A duty is a task involved in it; a skill is an ability used to perform tasks; a qualification records a recognised educational or training achievement. These ideas are connected but not identical. Training routes and entry rules vary and may change. This lesson teaches how to ask about them, not current professional eligibility. Check exact requirements with the appropriate institution before making a real application.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original dialogue: Ayuk asks what a laboratory worker does. Bih explains that duties may involve careful record-keeping and following supervised procedures. Ayuk asks which training route the relevant institution recognises. Bih says they should check the institution's current information rather than assume that liking science alone qualifies someone to practise.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Describe work with precise verbs, then ask how a skill relates to a duty. Distinguish general interest from a formal requirement. In a written comparison, avoid promising employment, a salary or admission. Label fictional examples clearly.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **occupation**: a type of work a person does
- **duty**: a task or responsibility within a job
- **skill**: an ability used to carry out work
- **qualification**: a recognised educational or training achievement

- An imaginary character considers teaching as a career.
- An imaginary worker keeps accurate laboratory records.
- Careful observation helps a worker notice relevant changes.

Common mistake: “An occupation is always a person's favourite school subject.” → “occupation: a type of work a person does.”. A subject interest can inform a choice but is not itself an occupation.

**Card ce3-21-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.

> _occupation: a type of work a person does._  
> _duty: a task or responsibility within a job._  
> _skill: an ability used to carry out work._  
> _qualification: a recognised educational or training achievement._

## Lesson 22: Writing - Expository - Choose a profession and write on the advantages and disadvantages

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-22 (Expository writing: a profession's advantages and challenges)

**Note** (draft)

> Expository writing explains a topic through organised points, reasons and examples. Introduce the profession, then discuss advantages and challenges fairly. Distinguish facts you have checked from personal preferences. Training, working conditions and entry requirements can differ by place and change over time, so do not invent a guaranteed qualification route or income. End by bringing the explanation together rather than adding an unrelated claim.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> Expository writing explains a topic through organised points, reasons and examples. Introduce the profession, then discuss advantages and challenges fairly. Distinguish facts you have checked from personal preferences. Training, working conditions and entry requirements can differ by place and change over time, so do not invent a guaranteed qualification route or income. End by bringing the explanation together rather than adding an unrelated claim.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original model: Teaching can allow a person to help learners understand new ideas. It also requires preparation, patience and continued learning. A teacher may find progress rewarding, while managing a busy class can be challenging. The training and registration requirements should be checked with an appropriate institution. The profession therefore needs both interest in learners and a realistic understanding of its responsibilities.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Make a plan with introduction, advantages, challenges and conclusion. Support each point with an explanation rather than praise or criticism alone. Avoid treating one person's preference as a universal fact. A balanced account can still reach a reasoned personal view.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **explanation**: a point developed with reasons and examples
- **advantage**: a potential positive feature of an occupation
- **challenge**: a difficulty or demand that should be considered
- **balanced conclusion**: an ending reflecting the points actually discussed

- The writer explains why lesson preparation takes time.
- Helping a learner understand can be rewarding.
- Managing several tasks requires organisation.

Common mistake: “A list of unsupported adjectives is a fully developed explanation.” → “explanation: a point developed with reasons and examples.”. Explain how or why a point holds rather than listing labels.

**Card ce3-22-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _explanation: a point developed with reasons and examples._  
> _advantage: a potential positive feature of an occupation._  
> _challenge: a difficulty or demand that should be considered._  
> _balanced conclusion: an ending reflecting the points actually discussed._

  _↳ feedback if chosen: challenge means a difficulty or demand that should be considered; the question instead describes a potential positive feature of an occupation._
- ✅ advantage
- ◻️ balanced conclusion  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: balanced conclusion means an ending reflecting the points actually discussed; the question instead describes a potential positive feature of an occupation._
- ◻️ explanation  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: explanation means a point developed with reasons and examples; the question instead describes a potential positive feature of an occupation._

> A potential positive feature of an occupation.  
> So the answer is “advantage”.

## Lesson 23: Speaking & Listening -Listen to a text on how a cash crop is cultivated and marketed

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-23 (Reading about cash-crop production and marketing)

**Note** (draft)

> A process account explains stages in a suitable order. Production concerns growing and preparing a crop; marketing concerns finding buyers and arranging a sale; transport connects locations. Sequence words such as first, then and finally help readers follow the account. A language lesson can explain these relationships without supplying agricultural treatment instructions or invented prices. Conditions differ by crop, season and place, so the passage is an original simplified example.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> A process account explains stages in a suitable order. Production concerns growing and preparing a crop; marketing concerns finding buyers and arranging a sale; transport connects locations. Sequence words such as first, then and finally help readers follow the account. A language lesson can explain these relationships without supplying agricultural treatment instructions or invented prices. Conditions differ by crop, season and place, so the passage is an original simplified example.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original passage: In an imaginary cocoa-growing community, growers prepare their produce and check its condition. They arrange transport to a collection point, where a buyer discusses quality and sale terms. The growers record the transaction and compare receipts with costs. A sale produces revenue, but transport and other costs affect what remains. The passage supplies no real market price or crop-treatment recipe.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Identify the main process and underline sequence signals. Distinguish a step from its purpose. When summarising, preserve both production and selling rather than reducing the process to receiving money. Do not add pesticide instructions or unverified statistics to the passage.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **production**: the growing and preparation of produce
- **marketing**: activities connecting produce with buyers and sale terms
- **transport**: moving produce between locations
- **transaction record**: a written account of an exchange

- Growers prepare cocoa produce for the next stage.
- Growers discuss quality and sale terms with a buyer.
- A vehicle carries prepared produce to a collection point.

Common mistake: “Production means only receiving money from a buyer.” → “production: the growing and preparation of produce.”. Growing and preparation precede or support the sale.

**Card ce3-23-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _production: the growing and preparation of produce._  
> _marketing: activities connecting produce with buyers and sale terms._  
> _transport: moving produce between locations._  
> _transaction record: a written account of an exchange._

- ◻️ transport

- ◻️ production
