## Lesson 24: Grammar - Quantifiers

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-24 (Quantifiers)

**Note** (draft)

> Quantifiers express amounts or numbers. Many and a few commonly go with plural countable nouns; much and a little with uncountable nouns. Some and a lot of can work with both, subject to context. A few suggests some, whereas few emphasises not many; a little suggests some, whereas little emphasises a shortage. Use the noun's meaning in context: paper as a material differs from papers as documents. Avoid treating all nouns as countable merely because they refer to real objects.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> Quantifiers express amounts or numbers. Many and a few commonly go with plural countable nouns; much and a little with uncountable nouns. Some and a lot of can work with both, subject to context. A few suggests some, whereas few emphasises not many; a little suggests some, whereas little emphasises a shortage. Use the noun's meaning in context: paper as a material differs from papers as documents. Avoid treating all nouns as countable merely because they refer to real objects.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> The club has a few books and a little time. It does not have much money, but many members can help. This uses books and members as countable, money and time as uncountable in these sentences. Changing a few to few can change the writer's emphasis even when both sentences are grammatical.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Identify the noun and decide how it is being used. Choose the quantifier that fits both its countability and the intended emphasis. Check the rest of the sentence rather than accepting a word just because it is familiar.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **many**: a quantifier used with plural countable nouns
- **much**: a quantifier used with uncountable nouns
- **a few**: a quantifier indicating some countable items
- **a little**: a quantifier indicating some uncountable amount

- Many pupils joined the club.
- The club does not have much time.
- A few books are available.

Common mistake: “Many money is the neutral standard phrase.” → “many: a quantifier used with plural countable nouns.”. Money is uncountable in this use; many belongs with plural countable nouns.

**Card ce3-24-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _many: a quantifier used with plural countable nouns._  
> _much: a quantifier used with uncountable nouns._  
> _a few: a quantifier indicating some countable items._  
> _a little: a quantifier indicating some uncountable amount._  

Check question: `e3-24-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means: a quantifier indicating some uncountable amount ?

- ✅ a little
- ◻️ many  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: many means a quantifier used with plural countable nouns; the question instead describes a quantifier indicating some uncountable amount._
- ◻️ a few  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: a few means a quantifier indicating some countable items; the question instead describes a quantifier indicating some uncountable amount._
- ◻️ much  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: much means a quantifier used with uncountable nouns; the question instead describes a quantifier indicating some uncountable amount._

Full working after a second miss:

> A quantifier indicating some uncountable amount.  
> So the answer is “a little”.  

**Card ce3-24-cases** (draft)

## Lesson 25: Writing: Article - The importance of cash crops to your my community

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-25 (An article on cash crops and community life)

**Note** (draft)

> An article informs a particular audience. Give it a suitable title, an introduction and organised paragraphs. Explain how a cash crop can contribute to income, jobs or trade, while recognising production costs, price uncertainty and possible effects on land use. Do not claim that every farmer earns the same income or that a crop removes all economic problems. Use original examples and checked facts rather than copying a source or inventing statistics.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> An article informs a particular audience. Give it a suitable title, an introduction and organised paragraphs. Explain how a cash crop can contribute to income, jobs or trade, while recognising production costs, price uncertainty and possible effects on land use. Do not claim that every farmer earns the same income or that a crop removes all economic problems. Use original examples and checked facts rather than copying a source or inventing statistics.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original model: Cash Crops and Our Community. An imaginary cocoa-growing community earns income when produce is sold. Transport and trading also create work. However, costs and changing prices affect what households retain. Food production and careful land use remain important. The community benefits most when people consider both opportunities and difficulties rather than assuming that every sale is pure profit.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> State the audience and main idea. Group related benefits and limitations in separate paragraphs and link them with clear connectives. Label invented examples as examples. Avoid turning an English article into unverified advice about crop treatment or investment.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **article title**: a concise heading indicating the article's subject
- **supporting example**: a case illustrating a point in the article
- **qualification**: a limit preventing an overgeneral claim
- **audience**: the people for whom the article is written

- Cash Crops and Our Community introduces the topic.
- Transport work illustrates a possible link to local employment.
- The article notes that prices and costs can change.

Common mistake: “A title must replace every paragraph of the article.” → “article title: a concise heading indicating the article's subject.”. The title announces the subject; the body develops it.

**Card ce3-25-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _article title: a concise heading indicating the article's subject._  
> _supporting example: a case illustrating a point in the article._  
> _qualification: a limit preventing an overgeneral claim._  
> _audience: the people for whom the article is written._  

Check question: `e3-25-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means: a limit preventing an overgeneral claim ?

- ◻️ supporting example  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: supporting example means a case illustrating a point in the article; the question instead describes a limit preventing an overgeneral claim._
- ✅ qualification
- ◻️ audience  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: audience means the people for whom the article is written; the question instead describes a limit preventing an overgeneral claim._
- ◻️ article title  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: article title means a concise heading indicating the article's subject; the question instead describes a limit preventing an overgeneral claim._

Full working after a second miss:

> A limit preventing an overgeneral claim.  
> So the answer is “qualification”.  

**Card ce3-25-cases** (draft)

> Classify a case by its features, rather than by a familiar word alone.
>
> _Cash Crops and Our Community introduces the topic._  
> _article title: a concise heading indicating the article's subject._

## Lesson 26: Speaking & Reading - Talk about the importance of managing people, time and money

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-26 (Discussing the management of people, time and money)

**Note** (draft)

> Planning assigns tasks, orders activities and checks resources. Delegation gives a suitable task to someone with a clear understanding of it; coordination helps related tasks fit together. A deadline states when work is due. A budget compares expected resources with planned uses. None guarantees success without checking progress. This lesson uses a fictional club rather than advising on a real household's finances or giving pupils authority over others.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> Planning assigns tasks, orders activities and checks resources. Delegation gives a suitable task to someone with a clear understanding of it; coordination helps related tasks fit together. A deadline states when work is due. A budget compares expected resources with planned uses. None guarantees success without checking progress. This lesson uses a fictional club rather than advising on a real household's finances or giving pupils authority over others.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original dialogue: A club plans an exhibition. Bih asks who will label the displays, while Ayuk checks that labels are ready before visitors arrive. Members compare expected contributions with planned material costs. They reserve time to review progress and adjust a task if someone needs help. Giving orders without checking understanding would not provide the same coordination.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Identify the goal, tasks, responsible people, timing and resources. Ask a clear question when an instruction is uncertain. Explain how one task depends on another. A plan should be reviewed when circumstances change rather than treated as a guarantee.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **delegation**: assigning a suitable task with clear responsibility
- **coordination**: arranging related activities so they fit together
- **deadline**: the time by which an activity should be completed
- **budget**: a plan comparing resources with proposed uses

- Bih agrees to label the exhibition displays.
- Labels are prepared before visitors need them.
- Displays must be ready before the exhibition opens.

Common mistake: “Delegation means assigning every task to nobody.” → “delegation: assigning a suitable task with clear responsibility.”. A task needs an identified responsible person and clear instructions.

**Card ce3-26-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _delegation: assigning a suitable task with clear responsibility._  
> _coordination: arranging related activities so they fit together._  
> _deadline: the time by which an activity should be completed._  
> _budget: a plan comparing resources with proposed uses._  

Check question: `e3-26-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means: assigning a suitable task with clear responsibility ?

- ✅ delegation
- ◻️ deadline  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: deadline means the time by which an activity should be completed; the question instead describes assigning a suitable task with clear responsibility._
- ◻️ coordination  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: coordination means arranging related activities so they fit together; the question instead describes assigning a suitable task with clear responsibility._
- ◻️ budget  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: budget means a plan comparing resources with proposed uses; the question instead describes assigning a suitable task with clear responsibility._

Full working after a second miss:

> Assigning a suitable task with clear responsibility.  
> So the answer is “delegation”.  

**Card ce3-26-cases** (draft)

> Classify a case by its features, rather than by a familiar word alone.
>
> _Bih agrees to label the exhibition displays._  
> _delegation: assigning a suitable task with clear responsibility._  

Check question: `e3-26-cases` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Members compare expected contributions with material costs.
> Which term best fits this example?

- ◻️ deadline  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: deadline concerns the time by which an activity should be completed. This case instead illustrates a plan comparing resources with proposed uses._
- ◻️ delegation  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: delegation concerns assigning a suitable task with clear responsibility. This case instead illustrates a plan comparing resources with proposed uses._
- ✅ budget
- ◻️ coordination  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: coordination concerns arranging related activities so they fit together. This case instead illustrates a plan comparing resources with proposed uses._

Full working after a second miss:

> Members compare expected contributions with material costs. A plan comparing resources with proposed uses.  
> So the answer is “budget”.  

**Card ce3-26-repair** (draft)

> Check that a claim agrees with the rule or definition it uses.

## Lesson 27: Vocabulary - Prefixes of negation

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-27 (Prefixes of negation)

**Note** (draft)

> A prefix is added at the beginning of a word. Negative prefixes such as un-, in-, im-, il-, ir- and dis- can create an opposite or negative meaning, but their choice is lexical: not every prefix can attach to every word. Unhappy, incorrect, impossible, illegal, irregular and dishonest are examples. Check spelling and meaning in a dictionary. A word starting with the same letters need not contain a negative prefix. Do not invent a form simply by choosing the prefix that looks shortest.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> A prefix is added at the beginning of a word. Negative prefixes such as un-, in-, im-, il-, ir- and dis- can create an opposite or negative meaning, but their choice is lexical: not every prefix can attach to every word. Unhappy, incorrect, impossible, illegal, irregular and dishonest are examples. Check spelling and meaning in a dictionary. A word starting with the same letters need not contain a negative prefix. Do not invent a form simply by choosing the prefix that looks shortest.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> A club report may describe an incorrect date, an impossible deadline and a dishonest claim. Each prefix forms an established word. Replacing incorrect with uncorrect does not produce the expected standard adjective. The change can affect meaning, so a writer should reread the sentence rather than checking spelling alone.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Identify the base word, try the established negative form and check it in a dictionary. Notice patterns such as im- before some words beginning with p, but do not turn a useful pattern into a universal rule without exceptions. Practise the actual words in context.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **unhappy**: the established negative of happy
- **impossible**: the established negative of possible
- **illegal**: the established negative form of legal
- **dishonest**: the established negative form of honest

- The character feels unhappy after losing a notebook.
- The stated task cannot be done under its conditions.
- A dictionary exercise pairs legal with illegal.

Common mistake: “Inhappy is the expected standard negative adjective.” → “unhappy: the established negative of happy.”. The established word is unhappy; prefix choice cannot be guessed freely.

**Card ce3-27-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _unhappy: the established negative of happy._  
> _impossible: the established negative of possible._  
> _illegal: the established negative form of legal._  
> _dishonest: the established negative form of honest._

## Lesson 28: Writing - A schedule - Draw a schedule of after school activities

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-28 (An after-school schedule)

**Note** (draft)

> A timetable organises activities into realistic periods. Begin with fixed commitments, including school, travel, chores, meals and rest. Add study periods that match your priorities and available energy. Allow transitions and breaks. A timetable is a plan to review, not a claim that every learner has identical responsibilities or resources. Use headings and consistent time notation so another person can understand it.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> A timetable organises activities into realistic periods. Begin with fixed commitments, including school, travel, chores, meals and rest. Add study periods that match your priorities and available energy. Allow transitions and breaks. A timetable is a plan to review, not a claim that every learner has identical responsibilities or resources. Use headings and consistent time notation so another person can understand it.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original model: Draft schedule: After the journey home, I complete the agreed household task. I then revise one topic, take a short break and practise questions. After the evening meal, I check the next day's books. I leave time for rest and adjust the plan when a task takes longer.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> List fixed commitments before choosing optional tasks. Check that periods do not overlap and that the total fits the available time. Review the plan after trying it; do not promise that a timetable alone guarantees success.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **time slot**: a stated period assigned to an activity
- **priority**: an activity given importance when time is limited
- **buffer**: unallocated allowance for delays or transitions
- **review**: checking and adjusting a plan after use

- A learner sets aside an interval after chores for reading.
- Bih allocates revision time before optional recreation.
- The plan allows time to move from a chore to study.

Common mistake: “A timetable needs no indication of when an activity happens.” → “time slot: a stated period assigned to an activity.”. Time slots make the plan usable rather than an unsequenced wish list.

**Card ce3-28-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _time slot: a stated period assigned to an activity._  
> _priority: an activity given importance when time is limited._  
> _buffer: unallocated allowance for delays or transitions._  
> _review: checking and adjusting a plan after use._  

Check question: `e3-28-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

  _↳ feedback if chosen: buffer means unallocated allowance for delays or transitions; the question instead describes a stated period assigned to an activity._
- ✅ time slot
- ◻️ review  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: review means checking and adjusting a plan after use; the question instead describes a stated period assigned to an activity._
- ◻️ priority  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: priority means an activity given importance when time is limited; the question instead describes a stated period assigned to an activity._

Full working after a second miss:

> A stated period assigned to an activity.  
> So the answer is “time slot”.  

**Card ce3-28-cases** (draft)

>

> _time slot: a stated period assigned to an activity._

## Lesson 29: Writing - Write amounts/sums of money in figures and words

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-29 (Writing monetary amounts in words and figures)

**Note** (draft)

> Figures use numerals, while words write an amount in language. When a form includes both, they must express the same amount and currency. Read place value carefully, especially zeros. Check the format requested by the teacher or form; separators and word conventions can vary. The exercise uses invented CFA-franc amounts, not a real payment. Matching words and figures is more important than copying a long number without checking it.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> Figures use numerals, while words write an amount in language. When a form includes both, they must express the same amount and currency. Read place value carefully, especially zeros. Check the format requested by the teacher or form; separators and word conventions can vary. The exercise uses invented CFA-franc amounts, not a real payment. Matching words and figures is more important than copying a long number without checking it.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original model: An imaginary payment form gives the amount as 12 500 CFA francs and as twelve thousand five hundred CFA francs. The zeros and place values must agree in both versions. Reading only the first part of the phrase could change the amount. A learner should compare the full expression before signing any classroom model.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Group the amount by place value, write the words and reread them as a number. Keep the currency explicit. Do not add an extra zero or confuse hundreds with thousands. Ask the teacher which accepted number-word style to use in formal examination answers.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **figures**: a numerical written representation of an amount
- **amount in words**: a linguistic representation of the same value
- **currency label**: the unit identifying the money involved
- **place-value check**: verification that digits and zeros express the intended value

- A model form writes the amount using numerals.
- A form writes an amount as a number phrase.
- The model identifies CFA francs rather than leaving the unit unstated.

Common mistake: “Figures means the writer's personal signature.” → “figures: a numerical written representation of an amount.”. Figures represent the number; a signature identifies a writer.

**Card ce3-29-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _figures: a numerical written representation of an amount._  
> _amount in words: a linguistic representation of the same value._  
> _currency label: the unit identifying the money involved._  
> _place-value check: verification that digits and zeros express the intended value._

- ✅ place-value check

  _↳ feedback if chosen: currency label means the unit identifying the money involved; the question instead describes verification that digits and zeros express the intended value._
- ◻️ figures  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: figures means a numerical written representation of an amount; the question instead describes verification that digits and zeros express the intended value._

## Lesson 30: Grammar - The future simple tense with will/shall and expressing the future through the present simple tense

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-30 (Future reference: will, shall and present schedules)

**Note** (draft)

> Will plus a base verb can express predictions, decisions or promises. Shall can occur with I and we in formal usage and in offers or suggestions, but it is not required in every first-person future sentence. The present simple can refer to an arranged timetable: the bus leaves tomorrow. Add a time expression when needed to make the future reference clear. Do not add -s after will or assume that every future event must use will.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> Will plus a base verb can express predictions, decisions or promises. Shall can occur with I and we in formal usage and in offers or suggestions, but it is not required in every first-person future sentence. The present simple can refer to an arranged timetable: the bus leaves tomorrow. Add a time expression when needed to make the future reference clear. Do not add -s after will or assume that every future event must use will.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Bih says, I will bring the notebook. The club timetable says the meeting starts tomorrow afternoon. Ayuk asks, Shall we check the notice? These forms do different jobs: a promise, a scheduled event and a suggestion. The timetable example is fictional rather than a claim about a current transport service.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Identify the intended function before choosing a form. Keep the base verb after will and shall. For present-simple schedules, check subject agreement and the future time expression. Avoid teaching shall as the only possible form with I or we.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **will + base verb**: a form used for predictions decisions or promises
- **scheduled present**: present-simple form referring to a timetable event
- **shall suggestion**: an offer or suggestion with shall in this context
- **future time expression**: words locating an event after the present

- Bih will bring the notebook.
- The fictional club meeting starts tomorrow.
- Shall we check the notice together?

Common mistake: “Bih will brings the notebook.” → “will + base verb: a form used for predictions decisions or promises.”. The verb stays in its base form after will.

**Card ce3-30-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _will + base verb: a form used for predictions decisions or promises._  
> _scheduled present: present-simple form referring to a timetable event._  
> _shall suggestion: an offer or suggestion with shall in this context._  
> _future time expression: words locating an event after the present._  

Check question: `e3-30-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means: a form used for predictions decisions or promises ?

- ◻️ shall suggestion  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: shall suggestion means an offer or suggestion with shall in this context; the question instead describes a form used for predictions decisions or promises._
- ◻️ future time expression  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: future time expression means words locating an event after the present; the question instead describes a form used for predictions decisions or promises._
- ✅ will + base verb
- ◻️ scheduled present  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: scheduled present means present-simple form referring to a timetable event; the question instead describes a form used for predictions decisions or promises._

Full working after a second miss:

> A form used for predictions decisions or promises.  
> So the answer is “will + base verb”.  

**Card ce3-30-cases** (draft)

> Classify a case by its features, rather than by a familiar word alone.
>
> _Bih will bring the notebook._  
> _will + base verb: a form used for predictions decisions or promises._

## Lesson 31: Speaking & Vocabulary - Discuss the role of banks in business. Words and abbreviations related to banking

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-31 (Understanding banking vocabulary and abbreviations)

**Note** (draft)

> Bank-related language includes account, deposit, withdrawal and balance. Their meanings depend on the stated transaction and perspective. An abbreviation shortens a longer expression and should be expanded before use if its meaning is unfamiliar. Do not assume that every institution uses the same forms or charges. This is a fictional vocabulary exercise, not an instruction to open an account or a statement of current banking rules.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> Bank-related language includes account, deposit, withdrawal and balance. Their meanings depend on the stated transaction and perspective. An abbreviation shortens a longer expression and should be expanded before use if its meaning is unfamiliar. Do not assume that every institution uses the same forms or charges. This is a fictional vocabulary exercise, not an instruction to open an account or a statement of current banking rules.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original notice: An imaginary school savings exercise records a deposit as money added and a withdrawal as money taken out. The balance records the amount remaining in the model account at that point. The label ATM expands to automated teller machine. The passage does not give permission to use another person's card or request real personal banking details.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Read from the account holder's stated perspective and check whether a movement adds or removes money. Expand an unfamiliar abbreviation from an appropriate source. Never infer a real fee, interest rate or account condition from a classroom example.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **deposit**: money added to the stated model account
- **withdrawal**: money taken out of the stated model account
- **balance**: the amount remaining at the stated point
- **ATM**: the abbreviation for automated teller machine

- The record shows money paid into the imaginary account.
- The record shows money removed from the imaginary account.
- The model records what remains after its listed movements.

Common mistake: “A deposit in this model always removes money from the account.” → “deposit: money added to the stated model account.”. The model defines it as an addition.

**Card ce3-31-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _deposit: money added to the stated model account._  
> _withdrawal: money taken out of the stated model account._  
> _balance: the amount remaining at the stated point._  
> _ATM: the abbreviation for automated teller machine._  

Check question: `e3-31-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means: money added to the stated model account ?

- ◻️ balance  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: balance means the amount remaining at the stated point; the question instead describes money added to the stated model account._
- ◻️ ATM  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: ATM means the abbreviation for automated teller machine; the question instead describes money added to the stated model account._
- ◻️ withdrawal  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: withdrawal means money taken out of the stated model account; the question instead describes money added to the stated model account._
- ✅ deposit

Full working after a second miss:

> Money added to the stated model account.  
> So the answer is “deposit”.  

**Card ce3-31-cases** (draft)

> Classify a case by its features, rather than by a familiar word alone.

> _deposit: money added to the stated model account._

## Lesson 32: Writing - Fill/write a cheque/ money order

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-32 (Preparing a model cheque or payment order)

**Note** (draft)

> A payment form may request a date, payee, amount in figures, amount in words and an authorised signature. Exact layouts and rules differ by institution. Use a fictional classroom model and never supply real account information. The payee is the person or organisation to be paid. Words and figures must agree. A filled model is not a valid payment and does not demonstrate that funds are available. The teacher should confirm the examination's expected format.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> A payment form may request a date, payee, amount in figures, amount in words and an authorised signature. Exact layouts and rules differ by institution. Use a fictional classroom model and never supply real account information. The payee is the person or organisation to be paid. Words and figures must agree. A filled model is not a valid payment and does not demonstrate that funds are available. The teacher should confirm the examination's expected format.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original model: Classroom model only. Date: 23 October 2026. Payee: Imaginary School Book Club. Figures: 12 500 CFA francs. Words: twelve thousand five hundred CFA francs. Writer: an imaginary authorised signatory. No real account number or signature is included.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Identify each field before entering a value. Compare the two amount forms and check the recipient's name. Do not sign for another person or treat a copied sample as a real financial instruction. This exercise assesses form-reading and writing language.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **payee**: the named person or organisation to receive payment
- **date field**: the field stating the payment document's date
- **matching amounts**: words and figures expressing the same value
- **authorised signatory**: a person entitled to sign in the stated arrangement

- The model names the Imaginary School Book Club as recipient.
- A classroom model labels the document's date.
- The model's written number phrase agrees with its figures.

Common mistake: “The payee is always the bank's building address.” → “payee: the named person or organisation to receive payment.”. The payee identifies the recipient, not a building location.

> _authorised signatory: a person entitled to sign in the stated arrangement._

## Lesson 33: Speaking & Reading - Discuss various means of transportation bringing out their advantages and disadvantages

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-33 (Comparing means of transport)

**Note** (draft)

> A comparison needs a purpose and criteria. Distance, cost, travel time, accessibility and the nature of a load can affect a choice. One means of transport is not automatically best for every journey. Use comparatives with a clear basis and qualify claims when evidence is limited. The original passage presents conditions for discussion; it does not supply current fares or route availability.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> A comparison needs a purpose and criteria. Distance, cost, travel time, accessibility and the nature of a load can affect a choice. One means of transport is not automatically best for every journey. Use comparatives with a clear basis and qualify claims when evidence is limited. The original passage presents conditions for discussion; it does not supply current fares or route availability.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original passage: An imaginary club must carry bulky displays to a nearby hall. Walking suits members carrying light personal bags, but the bulky items need suitable arranged transport. A distant journey raises different questions about time, cost and access. The club checks the actual conditions before choosing rather than assuming that the fastest option on one route is best everywhere.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Identify the traveller's goal and constraints. Compare the same feature across alternatives and explain a trade-off. Avoid unsupported universal claims such as buses are always fastest. State what further information would be needed for a real decision.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **criterion**: a feature used to compare options
- **constraint**: a condition limiting feasible choices
- **trade-off**: giving up one advantage to gain another
- **qualified comparison**: a comparison limited to its evidence and setting

- The club compares transport by load capacity.
- Bulky displays cannot be carried like light personal bags.
- A cheaper option may require a longer journey.

Common mistake: “A criterion is merely a random unrelated word.” → “criterion: a feature used to compare options.”. It is a relevant basis for comparison.

**Card ce3-33-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _criterion: a feature used to compare options._  
> _constraint: a condition limiting feasible choices._  
> _trade-off: giving up one advantage to gain another._  
> _qualified comparison: a comparison limited to its evidence and setting._  

Check question: `e3-33-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means: a comparison limited to its evidence and setting ?

- ✅ qualified comparison
- ◻️ criterion  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: criterion means a feature used to compare options; the question instead describes a comparison limited to its evidence and setting._
- ◻️ trade-off  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: trade-off means giving up one advantage to gain another; the question instead describes a comparison limited to its evidence and setting._
- ◻️ constraint  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: constraint means a condition limiting feasible choices; the question instead describes a comparison limited to its evidence and setting._

Full working after a second miss:

> A comparison limited to its evidence and setting.  
> So the answer is “qualified comparison”.  

**Card ce3-33-cases** (draft)

> Classify a case by its features, rather than by a familiar word alone.

## Lesson 34: Grammar - Prepositions- Prepositions of, movement, place and manner in relation to travelling

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-34 (Prepositions of place, movement and manner)

**Note** (draft)

> Prepositions show relationships. At and in can locate a person; to commonly marks a destination; into and out of express movement across a boundary. Through describes movement within and across something; across often describes movement from one side to another. Travel by bus differs from on foot. Context determines the phrase, and a preposition's meaning cannot be learned by replacing every one with to. Use complete examples and check the relationship intended.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> Prepositions show relationships. At and in can locate a person; to commonly marks a destination; into and out of express movement across a boundary. Through describes movement within and across something; across often describes movement from one side to another. Travel by bus differs from on foot. Context determines the phrase, and a preposition's meaning cannot be learned by replacing every one with to. Use complete examples and check the relationship intended.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Bih waits at the bus stop, travels to Buea and walks into a room. She travels by bus but goes on foot for the short final walk. The phrases identify position, destination, boundary crossing and manner. They are original model sentences, not instructions to travel without an adult or a real timetable.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Ask whether the sentence describes location, direction or method. Choose a phrase that matches the whole sentence. Do not confuse into, which shows movement, with in, which can describe a position. Check fixed expressions such as on foot.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **location**: where a person or object is
- **destination**: the endpoint towards which movement is directed
- **boundary crossing**: movement from outside to inside or inside to outside
- **travel manner**: the means or method of a journey

- Bih is at the bus stop.
- Ayuk travels to Buea.
- Bih walks into the reading room.

Common mistake: “At the stop necessarily means she has already travelled to another town.” → “location: where a person or object is.”. The phrase locates her; it does not state a journey's destination.

**Card ce3-34-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _location: where a person or object is._  
> _destination: the endpoint towards which movement is directed._  
> _boundary crossing: movement from outside to inside or inside to outside._  
> _travel manner: the means or method of a journey._  

Check question: `e3-34-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means: movement from outside to inside or inside to outside ?

- ◻️ destination  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: destination means the endpoint towards which movement is directed; the question instead describes movement from outside to inside or inside to outside._
- ✅ boundary crossing
- ◻️ travel manner  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: travel manner means the means or method of a journey; the question instead describes movement from outside to inside or inside to outside._
- ◻️ location  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: location means where a person or object is; the question instead describes movement from outside to inside or inside to outside._

Full working after a second miss:

> Movement from outside to inside or inside to outside.  
> So the answer is “boundary crossing”.  

**Card ce3-34-cases** (draft)

> Classify a case by its features, rather than by a familiar word alone.
>
> _Bih is at the bus stop._  
> _location: where a person or object is._

  _↳ feedback if chosen: travel manner concerns the means or method of a journey. This case instead illustrates the endpoint towards which movement is directed._
- ◻️ location  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: location concerns where a person or object is. This case instead illustrates the endpoint towards which movement is directed._
- ✅ destination

> travel manner → Ngozi travels by bus and then continues on foot.

## Lesson 35: Writing - Narrative - Give an account of a journey you undertook by a particular means of transport

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-35 (Narrating a journey)

**Note** (draft)

> A journey narrative gives a reader a starting situation, a route, significant events and an ending. Use time connectives and a consistent narrative viewpoint. Describe a few relevant details instead of listing every object seen. Past tenses commonly tell the completed events, while the past continuous can provide background. A story should not promise that an unsafe action is sensible. Invent a journey if you prefer not to share personal travel details.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> A journey narrative gives a reader a starting situation, a route, significant events and an ending. Use time connectives and a consistent narrative viewpoint. Describe a few relevant details instead of listing every object seen. Past tenses commonly tell the completed events, while the past continuous can provide background. A story should not promise that an unsafe action is sensible. Invent a journey if you prefer not to share personal travel details.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original model: Last week, an imaginary learner travelled with an aunt from a village to Buea. They waited at a sheltered stop while rain fell. When the bus arrived, they boarded carefully and stored the bag safely. The rain eased on the way, and the learner noticed fields beyond the window. At their destination, the aunt checked the bag before they left the bus.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Plan departure, an event on the way and arrival. Check the order and who each pronoun refers to. Use description to support the story rather than replacing the sequence. End with the journey's outcome or a relevant reflection.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **starting situation**: the opening circumstances of a narrative
- **background action**: an ongoing circumstance around a main event
- **chronological order**: events arranged in their time sequence
- **resolution**: the outcome closing the journey account

- The learner and an aunt prepare to travel to Buea.
- Rain was falling while they waited.
- Waiting precedes boarding and arrival follows the journey.

Common mistake: “A beginning should assume the reader already knows every traveller and destination.” → “starting situation: the opening circumstances of a narrative.”. Introduce enough context for the reader to follow the events.

**Card ce3-35-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _starting situation: the opening circumstances of a narrative._  
> _background action: an ongoing circumstance around a main event._  
> _chronological order: events arranged in their time sequence._  
> _resolution: the outcome closing the journey account._  

Check question: `e3-35-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means: the opening circumstances of a narrative ?

- ✅ starting situation
- ◻️ chronological order  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: chronological order means events arranged in their time sequence; the question instead describes the opening circumstances of a narrative._
- ◻️ background action  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: background action means an ongoing circumstance around a main event; the question instead describes the opening circumstances of a narrative._
- ◻️ resolution  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: resolution means the outcome closing the journey account; the question instead describes the opening circumstances of a narrative._

Full working after a second miss:

> The opening circumstances of a narrative.  
> So the answer is “starting situation”.  

**Card ce3-35-cases** (draft)

> Classify a case by its features, rather than by a familiar word alone.
>
> _The learner and an aunt prepare to travel to Buea._  
> _starting situation: the opening circumstances of a narrative._

## Lesson 36: Speaking & Listening -Share ideas on sex education

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-36 (Preparing a respectful discussion of sexual health)

**Note** (draft)

> A responsible classroom discussion uses accurate terms, respect and age-appropriate information. Consent, privacy, reliable sources and seeking appropriate help are useful discussion themes. Health status does not determine a person's worth. The exercise uses fictional situations and requires no personal disclosures. This is written preparation for a language discussion, not a diagnosis or a substitute for advice from trained health professionals.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> A responsible classroom discussion uses accurate terms, respect and age-appropriate information. Consent, privacy, reliable sources and seeking appropriate help are useful discussion themes. Health status does not determine a person's worth. The exercise uses fictional situations and requires no personal disclosures. This is written preparation for a language discussion, not a diagnosis or a substitute for advice from trained health professionals.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original dialogue: Bih asks how to check a health message shared online. Ayuk suggests comparing it with an established health source and asking a trained professional when unsure. Another speaker adds that nobody should be pressured to describe private experiences. They agree to discuss claims respectfully and avoid spreading rumours about classmates.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Separate a question about information from a request for private details. Give a reason when judging a source: popularity alone does not establish accuracy. Use neutral wording and encourage appropriate support rather than shame. Teachers should review discussion suitability for the class before oral practice.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **reliable information**: information checked against credible evidence and appropriate expertise
- **privacy**: respect for a person's control over private information
- **consent**: freely given agreement that should be respected
- **trained support**: help from an appropriate qualified professional

- A learner compares a health claim with an established health source.
- The discussion uses fictional cases without demanding disclosure.
- A speaker asks before sharing another person's personal story.

Common mistake: “A rumour becomes medically accurate because many people share it.” → “reliable information: information checked against credible evidence and appropriate expertise.”. Popularity is not evidence of medical accuracy.

**Card ce3-36-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _reliable information: information checked against credible evidence and appropriate expertise._  
> _privacy: respect for a person's control over private information._  
> _consent: freely given agreement that should be respected._  
> _trained support: help from an appropriate qualified professional._  

Check question: `e3-36-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

- ◻️ privacy  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: privacy means respect for a person's control over private information; the question instead describes freely given agreement that should be respected._
- ◻️ trained support  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: trained support means help from an appropriate qualified professional; the question instead describes freely given agreement that should be respected._
- ◻️ reliable information  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: reliable information means information checked against credible evidence and appropriate expertise; the question instead describes freely given agreement that should be respected._

Full working after a second miss:

> Freely given agreement that should be respected.  
> So the answer is “consent”.  

**Card ce3-36-cases** (draft)

> Classify a case by its features, rather than by a familiar word alone.
>

- ◻️ trained support: help from an appropriate qualified professional.  
  _↳ explanation: This statement is correct: trained support means help from an appropriate qualified professional. Look for a claim that conflicts with its definition._
- ◻️ reliable information: information checked against credible evidence and appropriate expertise.  
  _↳ explanation: This statement is correct: reliable information means information checked against credible evidence and appropriate expertise. Look for a claim that conflicts with its definition._
- ✅ A language task requires every pupil to reveal private experiences.  
  _↳ explanation: A learner can participate through fictional or general examples._
- ◻️ privacy: respect for a person's control over private information.  
  _↳ explanation: This statement is correct: privacy means respect for a person's control over private information. Look for a claim that conflicts with its definition._

Full working after a second miss:

> The wrong statement is “A language task requires every pupil to reveal private experiences.”.  
> A learner can participate through fictional or general examples.  

## Lesson 37: Grammar - Correlating conjunctions

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-37 (Correlative conjunctions)

**Note** (draft)

> Correlative conjunctions work in pairs: both ... and, either ... or, neither ... nor and not only ... but also. They join parallel structures and express different relationships. Both includes the alternatives; either offers an alternative; neither excludes them. Put the pair around matching grammatical units. Subject-verb agreement can become complex when subjects differ, so the introductory examples use unambiguous pairs. Do not combine either with nor or use a negative pair as if it meant both.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> Correlative conjunctions work in pairs: both ... and, either ... or, neither ... nor and not only ... but also. They join parallel structures and express different relationships. Both includes the alternatives; either offers an alternative; neither excludes them. Put the pair around matching grammatical units. Subject-verb agreement can become complex when subjects differ, so the introductory examples use unambiguous pairs. Do not combine either with nor or use a negative pair as if it meant both.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Both Bih and Ayuk joined the club includes them. Neither Bih nor Ayuk was absent excludes each from the absence claim. The club can meet either indoors or outdoors presents alternatives. Not only reading but also discussion names related activities. Keep the same kind of structure on each side of a pair.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Choose the relationship before filling a pair. Check that nouns join nouns or corresponding phrases join corresponding phrases. Read the complete sentence to avoid an unintended double negative. More complex mixed-number agreement should be checked with the teacher's course convention.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **both ... and**: a pair including the linked alternatives
- **either ... or**: a pair presenting alternatives
- **neither ... nor**: a pair excluding the linked alternatives
- **parallel structure**: matching grammatical forms in linked positions

- Both Bih and Ayuk attended.
- Meet either in the hall or in the library.
- Neither Bih nor Ayuk was absent.

Common mistake: “Both ... and excludes each person from the action.” → “both ... and: a pair including the linked alternatives.”. The pair includes both alternatives.

**Card ce3-37-terms** (draft)

> Distinguish the lesson's concepts before applying them.
>
> _both ... and: a pair including the linked alternatives._  
> _either ... or: a pair presenting alternatives._  
> _neither ... nor: a pair excluding the linked alternatives._  
> _parallel structure: matching grammatical forms in linked positions._  

Check question: `e3-37-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means: a pair presenting alternatives ?

- ✅ either ... or
- ◻️ both ... and  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: both ... and means a pair including the linked alternatives; the question instead describes a pair presenting alternatives._
- ◻️ neither ... nor  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: neither ... nor means a pair excluding the linked alternatives; the question instead describes a pair presenting alternatives._
- ◻️ parallel structure  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: parallel structure means matching grammatical forms in linked positions; the question instead describes a pair presenting alternatives._

Full working after a second miss:

> A pair presenting alternatives.  
> So the answer is “either ... or”.  

**Card ce3-37-cases** (draft)

> Classify a case by its features, rather than by a familiar word alone.
>
> _Both Bih and Ayuk attended._  
> _both ... and: a pair including the linked alternatives._

## Lesson 38: Vocabulary - Spelling rules

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-38 (Spelling: endings and word families)

**Note** (draft)

> Spelling changes depend on the word and the ending. A consonant plus y often changes to i before certain endings: study becomes studied. A final silent e is often dropped before -ing: write becomes writing. Some short stressed verbs double a final consonant: stop becomes stopping. These are patterns with conditions, not universal rules. Use a dictionary for doubtful forms and distinguish a spelling change from a change of tense or word class.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> Spelling changes depend on the word and the ending. A consonant plus y often changes to i before certain endings: study becomes studied. A final silent e is often dropped before -ing: write becomes writing. Some short stressed verbs double a final consonant: stop becomes stopping. These are patterns with conditions, not universal rules. Use a dictionary for doubtful forms and distinguish a spelling change from a change of tense or word class.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Bih revises a journal containing studied, writing and stopping. She identifies the base word and the ending before making a correction. Playing keeps the y because it follows a vowel; studied changes y after a consonant. Copying a rule without its condition can therefore create new errors.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Separate base and suffix. Check the final letters and, where needed, stress. Compare with a trusted dictionary rather than inventing a new spelling from sound alone. A correct spelling should still fit the sentence's tense and meaning.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **consonant-y change**: changing final consonant plus y before certain endings
- **silent-e dropping**: removing final silent e before an ending such as -ing
- **conditional doubling**: doubling a final consonant in suitable stressed short forms
- **vowel-y retention**: keeping y after a vowel in a form such as played

- Study becomes studied.
- Write becomes writing.
- Stop becomes stopping.

Common mistake: “Studyed is the standard past spelling.” → “consonant-y change: changing final consonant plus y before certain endings.”. Final consonant plus y changes to i in studied.

> _silent-e dropping: removing final silent e before an ending such as -ing._  
> _conditional doubling: doubling a final consonant in suitable stressed short forms._  
> _vowel-y retention: keeping y after a vowel in a form such as played._

## Lesson 39: Writing - Slogans - Write slogans on STIs

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e3-39 (Writing responsible slogans about sexual health)

**Note** (draft)

> A slogan is a brief memorable message for an audience. For health topics, it must also be accurate, respectful and free of stigma. A slogan cannot diagnose an infection or replace professional advice. Avoid false guarantees, blame or frightening claims. A useful message can encourage credible information, respectful choices and access to trained care. Discuss classroom suitability with the teacher; pupils do not need to reveal private experiences for this writing task.

**Learn: Understand the ideas** (draft)

> A slogan is a brief memorable message for an audience. For health topics, it must also be accurate, respectful and free of stigma. A slogan cannot diagnose an infection or replace professional advice. Avoid false guarantees, blame or frightening claims. A useful message can encourage credible information, respectful choices and access to trained care. Discuss classroom suitability with the teacher; pupils do not need to reveal private experiences for this writing task.

**Learn: Apply the ideas locally** (draft)

> Original model: Original model slogans: Get the facts; seek trained care. Respect privacy, support health. Ask a health professional, not a rumour. These messages encourage informed help-seeking without labelling or shaming a person. A teacher can discuss which wording is suitable for a school audience.

**Learn: Reason and check** (draft)

> Choose one action and express it concisely. Check the factual claim and the audience. Remove any suggestion that a person's worth depends on health status or that an English slogan guarantees prevention. Reread for rhythm and spelling without sacrificing accuracy.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **concise message**: a short focused statement the audience can remember
- **accuracy**: a claim consistent with reliable health information
- **non-stigmatising language**: wording respecting a person's dignity regardless of health status
- **appropriate help-seeking**: using trained support rather than relying on a slogan for diagnosis

- Get the facts; seek trained care gives one linked purpose.
- The writer avoids promising a guaranteed cure in a slogan.
- The message encourages support without insults or blame.

Common mistake: “A slogan must include an entire medical textbook.” → “concise message: a short focused statement the audience can remember.”. A slogan is brief; detailed information belongs in an appropriate supporting source.
